SAGE builds upon the DDIM algorithm and incorporates a novel guidance mechanism utilizing the self-attention layers of the diffusion U-Net. This mechanism computes a reconstruction objective based on attention maps generated during the inverse DDIM process, enabling efficient reconstruction of unedited regions without the need to precisely reconstruct the entire input image. Thus, SAGE directly addresses the key challenges in image editing. The superiority of SAGE over other methods is demonstrated through quantitative and qualitative evaluations and confirmed by a statistically validated comprehensive user study, in which all 47 surveyed users preferred SAGE over competing methods. Additionally, SAGE ranks as the top-performing method in seven out of 10 quantitative analyses and secures second and third places in the remaining three.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SAGE (Self-Attention Guidance for image Editing), a novel approach for prompt-based image editing using pre-trained diffusion models. Unlike existing methods that often struggle with computational efficiency and reconstruction quality, SAGE leverages intermediate self-attention maps computed during the inverse DDIM process. By incorporating a reconstruction objective based on these attention maps, SAGE can efficiently reconstruct unedited regions without needing to fully reconstruct the entire input image. The method uses a classifier-free guidance (CFG) and a novel reconstruction guidance mechanism that operates on the self-attention maps within the diffusion U-Net, achieving a fine balance between editing and preservation. Extensive quantitative, qualitative evaluations and a user study are performed to demonstrate the superiority of SAGE. The paper's key contributions are: a novel editing framework that utilizes intermediate noise vectors from the inverse DDIM process, a reconstruction guidance loss term based on self-attention layers, and experimental validation showing SAGE's superior performance against state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and important problem in the field of image editing: how to efficiently and effectively edit real images using text prompts and diffusion models.  The approach of using self-attention maps for guidance is interesting, providing a different angle to existing methods.  The paper highlights the limitations of existing methods.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using self-attention maps from the *inverse* DDIM process for guidance is a valuable new direction. This contrasts with methods that primarily focus on manipulating cross-attention maps or latent space embeddings. The combination of CFG and the reconstruction guidance mechanism operating on self-attention maps appears effective.
*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates a compelling trade-off between computational efficiency and reconstruction quality, which are key challenges in diffusion-based image editing.  The method appears to offer performance improvements over existing methods while using less memory.
*   **Experimental Validation:**  The paper provides thorough experimental validation, including quantitative metrics, qualitative results, ablation studies, and a user study.  The comprehensive comparisons against state-of-the-art methods strengthen the claims. The inclusion of the user study adds considerable weight, showing a clear user preference for SAGE.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the technical details of the proposed approach. The figures effectively illustrate the method and the experimental results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper acknowledges that SAGE, like other diffusion-based editing methods, is sensitive to hyperparameter settings. While the authors attempted to maintain consistent settings for comparisons, this inherent sensitivity could limit its practical applicability. It would be beneficial to include information for the best selection of hyperparameters for other datasets.
*   **Structure Preservation Limitations:** The paper acknowledges limitations in structure preservation when removing objects. The unintended filling of gaps with similar content highlights a potential conflict between CFG and self-attention guidance. This point needs more addressing, since object removal is an important editing function.
*   **Failure Cases:**  The description of failure cases is limited. The authors mention the production of oversaturated and poorly reconstructed images in a small fraction of cases.  More detailed analysis of these failure cases, along with potential solutions, would improve the paper's robustness.
*   **Limited Scope of Dataset:** The used dataset is relatively small and not as varied as some of the public image datasets. More comparison on publicly used datasets could boost the score and credibility of the paper.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the mentioned weaknesses, the paper presents a valuable and well-validated contribution to the field of prompt-based image editing. The novelty of using self-attention guidance from the inverse DDIM process, combined with the strong experimental results and user study, justify a significant score.

Score: 7.

The paper is a solid advancement, but its practical impact is somewhat tempered by its hyperparameter sensitivity and limitations regarding object removal. Addressing these aspects in future work could lead to an even stronger contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Ethics and Persuasion in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback: A Procedural Rhetorical Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09576v1)
- **Authors**: Shannon Lodoen, Alexi Orchard
- **Abstract**: Since 2022, versions of generative AI chatbots such as ChatGPT and Claude have been trained using a specialized technique called Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) to fine-tune language model output using feedback from human annotators. As a result, the integration of RLHF has greatly enhanced the outputs of these large language models (LLMs) and made the interactions and responses appear more "human-like" than those of previous versions using only supervised learning. The increasing convergence of human and machine-written text has potentially severe ethical, sociotechnical, and pedagogical implications relating to transparency, trust, bias, and interpersonal relations. To highlight these implications, this paper presents a rhetorical analysis of some of the central procedures and processes currently being reshaped by RLHF-enhanced generative AI chatbots: upholding language conventions, information seeking practices, and expectations for social relationships. Rhetorical investigations of generative AI and LLMs have, to this point, focused largely on the persuasiveness of the content generated. Using Ian Bogost's concept of procedural rhetoric, this paper shifts the site of rhetorical investigation from content analysis to the underlying mechanisms of persuasion built into RLHF-enhanced LLMs. In doing so, this theoretical investigation opens a new direction for further inquiry in AI ethics that considers how procedures rerouted through AI-driven technologies might reinforce hegemonic language use, perpetuate biases, decontextualize learning, and encroach upon human relationships. It will therefore be of interest to educators, researchers, scholars, and the growing number of users of generative AI chatbots.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Ethics and Persuasion in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback: A Procedural Rhetorical Approach" analyzes the persuasive aspects "baked into" large language models (LLMs) through Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Shifting from content analysis to the underlying mechanisms of persuasion, it employs Ian Bogost's procedural rhetoric to investigate how RLHF reshapes practices like language conventions, information seeking, and social relationships. It highlights ethical considerations related to transparency, trust, bias, and interpersonal relations, arguing that RLHF, while enhancing LLMs, also subtly reinforces hegemonic language, perpetuates biases, decontextualizes learning, and impacts human relationships.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of procedural rhetoric to analyze the *process* of RLHF in LLMs, rather than just analyzing the output. This is a shift from existing rhetoric-based research in AI, which often focuses on prompt engineering or output analysis. Analyzing the underlying mechanisms provides a deeper understanding of how LLMs exert influence. While procedural rhetoric itself isn't new, its specific application to LLM training is relatively unexplored in the humanities.

*   **Significance:** The significance comes from highlighting the subtle but pervasive influence of RLHF on shaping user behavior and beliefs. The paper effectively identifies key areas where RLHF-enhanced LLMs subtly reinforce certain values and assumptions related to language, information access, and social interaction. The discussions of bias, the blurring of human/machine distinction in both information sourcing and social interactions, and the potential for these effects to influence human relationships offer valuable insights.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Framework:** The use of procedural rhetoric provides a strong analytical lens for examining the persuasive power of technological processes.
    *   **Multidisciplinary Approach:** The paper effectively integrates insights from computer science, STS (Science and Technology Studies), and rhetoric, broadening the audience and impact.
    *   **Clear Identification of Ethical Concerns:** The paper articulates important ethical implications of RLHF, particularly regarding bias, transparency, and the potential for negative impacts on critical thinking and social skills.
    *   **Forward-Looking:** It identifies areas needing further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Empirical Evidence:** The paper is largely theoretical. While it draws on existing research, it lacks direct empirical investigation of the specific persuasive effects it describes. More user studies or case studies could strengthen its claims.
    *   **Generalizations:** The paper sometimes relies on generalizations about LLMs and RLHF. The specifics of different LLMs and training methodologies can vary significantly, potentially affecting the persuasive power. A more nuanced analysis, differentiating between different systems, would strengthen its arguments.
    *   **Overstating the Influence:** While the subtle persuasive effects are present, it is unclear whether they significantly impact users' behaviours and beliefs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   **Shifting Research Focus:** Encouraging other researchers to move beyond content analysis and examine the underlying mechanisms of persuasion in AI.
    *   **Informing Ethical Design:** Providing a framework for ethical design of AI systems that are more transparent and accountable.
    *   **Raising Awareness:** Educating users about the subtle persuasive effects of LLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a valuable contribution, but its lack of empirical grounding limits its overall impact. It proposes a novel analytical approach and raises crucial ethical questions. However, the claims it makes need to be backed up by more than a synthesis of exisiting literature. The theoretical framework would greatly benefit from direct engagement with user data and a more nuanced consideration of the diversity in LLM systems and training data sets.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### WorldView-Bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Global Cultural Perspectives in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09595v1)
- **Authors**: Abdullah Mushtaq, Imran Taj, Rafay Naeem, Ibrahim Ghaznavi, Junaid Qadir
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are predominantly trained and aligned in ways that reinforce Western-centric epistemologies and socio-cultural norms, leading to cultural homogenization and limiting their ability to reflect global civilizational plurality. Existing benchmarking frameworks fail to adequately capture this bias, as they rely on rigid, closed-form assessments that overlook the complexity of cultural inclusivity. To address this, we introduce WorldView-Bench, a benchmark designed to evaluate Global Cultural Inclusivity (GCI) in LLMs by analyzing their ability to accommodate diverse worldviews. Our approach is grounded in the Multiplex Worldview proposed by Senturk et al., which distinguishes between Uniplex models, reinforcing cultural homogenization, and Multiplex models, which integrate diverse perspectives. WorldView-Bench measures Cultural Polarization, the exclusion of alternative perspectives, through free-form generative evaluation rather than conventional categorical benchmarks. We implement applied multiplexity through two intervention strategies: (1) Contextually-Implemented Multiplex LLMs, where system prompts embed multiplexity principles, and (2) Multi-Agent System (MAS)-Implemented Multiplex LLMs, where multiple LLM agents representing distinct cultural perspectives collaboratively generate responses. Our results demonstrate a significant increase in Perspectives Distribution Score (PDS) entropy from 13% at baseline to 94% with MAS-Implemented Multiplex LLMs, alongside a shift toward positive sentiment (67.7%) and enhanced cultural balance. These findings highlight the potential of multiplex-aware AI evaluation in mitigating cultural bias in LLMs, paving the way for more inclusive and ethically aligned AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "WorldView-Bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Global Cultural Perspectives in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WorldView-Bench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate Global Cultural Inclusivity (GCI) in Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors argue that existing benchmarks fail to adequately capture cultural bias because they rely on closed-form assessments.  WorldView-Bench uses free-form generative evaluation and is grounded in the "Multiplex Worldview" framework. They implement and evaluate two intervention strategies: (1) Contextually-Implemented Multiplex LLMs (using multiplexity-aware prompts), and (2) Multi-Agent System (MAS)-Implemented Multiplex LLMs (using multiple LLM agents representing different cultural perspectives).  The results show that the MAS-implemented approach significantly increases perspective diversity and improves cultural balance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of several factors. First, the design of WorldView-Bench itself is a contribution. The shift from closed-form to free-form evaluation addresses a valid concern regarding existing cultural benchmarks. Framing the evaluation around the "Multiplex Worldview" is another novel aspect, providing a theoretical grounding for measuring cultural inclusivity. Finally, the multi-agent approach to generating more diverse responses is an interesting methodological innovation.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical and increasingly important problem: cultural bias in LLMs. If LLMs are to be deployed globally, it is essential to ensure they don't reinforce Western-centric perspectives or homogenize cultural viewpoints. The paper provides a concrete tool (WorldView-Bench) and methods (the multiplexity interventions) that can be used to assess and potentially mitigate these biases.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of cultural bias in LLMs and justifies the need for a new benchmark.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The "Multiplex Worldview" provides a solid theoretical framework for evaluating cultural inclusivity.
    *   **Methodological Rigor:** The data generation and validation pipeline for WorldView-Bench appears to be rigorous, incorporating both automated and manual quality assurance.
    *   **Empirical Results:** The results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed intervention strategies, particularly the MAS approach, in increasing perspective diversity.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The use of zero-shot classification for response categorization, metrics-based analysis (PDS), and sentiment analysis provides a multi-faceted evaluation.
    *   **Reproducibility**: The work also provides enough details to be reproducible.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Cultural Perspective Selection:** While the authors chose 7 cultural perspective categories, this selection is inherently subjective and might not fully represent the diversity of global cultures. A clearer justification for this specific choice would strengthen the work. Are these the main categories from the viewpoint of multiplexity or are they selected empirically for some other reason?
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Evaluation:**  The pipeline relies heavily on LLMs (GPT-40) for both cultural reference extraction and sentiment analysis. While GPT-40 is powerful, it's not immune to biases.  The paper acknowledges this in the limitations section but further analysis to address potential biases introduced by GPT-40 would be valuable.  Is there a way to validate the cultural reference extraction, especially for lesser-known traditions?
    *   **Scalability and Generalizability:** While the MAS approach shows promising results, it may be computationally expensive to implement at scale, depending on model selection. Also, more generalizability could be achieved if a wider variety of models are used.
    *   **Subjectivity of Sentiment Analysis:** Even with GPT-40, sentiment analysis in a cultural context remains subjective. "Neutral" sentiments can be interpreted in different ways.
    *   **Overemphasis on a Western-centric approach:** While the paper highlights a Western-centric tendency of LLMs, the benchmark and interventions are still influenced by Western academic framing and AI methodologies. The cultural contexts of other regions may require different evaluation and bias mitigation approaches.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of AI ethics and fairness by providing a concrete benchmark and methods for evaluating and mitigating cultural bias in LLMs. It could also inspire further research into more culturally sensitive AI development practices.

*   **Score Rationale:** While the paper has some weaknesses, its novelty and potential significance warrant a relatively high score. The WorldView-Bench benchmark and the MAS approach offer valuable contributions to addressing a critical problem. The weaknesses primarily relate to the subjectivity of cultural classifications and the potential for bias in the evaluation pipeline, which could be addressed in future work.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### How Hungry is AI? Benchmarking Energy, Water, and Carbon Footprint of LLM Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09598v1)
- **Authors**: Nidhal Jegham, Marwen Abdelatti, Lassad Elmoubarki, Abdeltawab Hendawi
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) spread across industries, understanding their environmental footprint at the inference level is no longer optional; it is essential. However, most existing studies exclude proprietary models, overlook infrastructural variability and overhead, or focus solely on training, even as inference increasingly dominates AI's environmental impact. To bridge this gap, this paper introduces a novel infrastructure-aware benchmarking framework for quantifying the environmental footprint of LLM inference across 30 state-of-the-art models as deployed in commercial data centers. Our framework combines public API performance data with region-specific environmental multipliers and statistical inference of hardware configurations. We additionally utilize cross-efficiency Data Envelopment Analysis (DEA) to rank models by performance relative to environmental cost. Our results show that o3 and DeepSeek-R1 emerge as the most energy-intensive models, consuming over 33 Wh per long prompt, more than 70 times the consumption of GPT-4.1 nano, and that Claude-3.7 Sonnet ranks highest in eco-efficiency. While a single short GPT-4o query consumes 0.43 Wh, scaling this to 700 million queries/day results in substantial annual environmental impacts. These include electricity use comparable to 35,000 U.S. homes, freshwater evaporation matching the annual drinking needs of 1.2 million people, and carbon emissions requiring a Chicago-sized forest to offset. These findings illustrate a growing paradox: although individual queries are efficient, their global scale drives disproportionate resource consumption. Our study provides a standardized, empirically grounded methodology for benchmarking the sustainability of LLM deployments, laying a foundation for future environmental accountability in AI development and sustainability standards.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework for benchmarking the energy, water, and carbon footprint of Large Language Model (LLM) inference in commercial data centers. The methodology combines publicly available API performance data with regional environmental multipliers (PUE, WUE, CIF) and statistical inference to estimate hardware configurations. The framework is applied to 30 LLMs, and Data Envelopment Analysis (DEA) is used to rank models based on performance relative to environmental cost.  The results demonstrate significant variations in environmental impact across models and highlight the growing scale of resource consumption due to LLM inference.  A case study of GPT-4o's annual environmental footprint illustrates substantial impacts, even with relatively efficient individual queries. The paper emphasizes the importance of infrastructure and presents DEA as a method for balancing capability with environmental costs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its integrated approach to benchmarking LLM inference. It goes beyond previous work by combining API performance data, infrastructure multipliers, and statistical hardware estimation. This allows for a more comprehensive assessment of environmental impact at the prompt level, including both proprietary and open-source models. The use of DEA to evaluate eco-efficiency is also a significant contribution. While individual components of the methodology exist, their combination and application to LLM inference represent a novel approach.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical and timely issue: the growing environmental footprint of AI. By providing a standardized methodology for benchmarking LLM deployments, the paper contributes to a more transparent and accountable AI ecosystem. The findings highlight the importance of considering infrastructure and deployment strategies, not just model architecture, when evaluating sustainability. The GPT-4o case study effectively illustrates the scale of resource consumption and the need for systemic solutions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Methodology:** The integration of API data, environmental multipliers, and hardware estimation provides a robust framework.
    *   **Broad Scope:** The analysis of 30 LLMs offers a valuable comparative assessment.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on commercial data centers and real-world deployment scenarios increases the paper's practical impact.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-organized and clearly explains the methodology and results.
    *   **Policy Implications:** The paper provides a strong basis for informing discussions on regulation and sustainability standards in AI.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Hardware Estimation Uncertainty:** The hardware estimation relies on statistical inference and assumptions, which introduce potential inaccuracies. Direct hardware telemetry data would be ideal, but is often unavailable.
    *   **Scope 3 Exclusion:** While the paper acknowledges Scope 3 emissions (embodied emissions from hardware), it excludes them due to data limitations. This simplifies the analysis but omits a significant component of the lifecycle impact.
    *   **Fixed Batch Size Assumption:** The assumption of a fixed batch size of 8 simplifies energy estimates but might not accurately represent dynamic batching in all deployment scenarios.
    *   **Regional Multiplier Dependence:** PUE, WUE, and CIF data varies significantly based on geography and data center. Using national averages when site-specific data is unavailable introduces error.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence AI development, sustainability standards, and policy decisions. It provides a valuable tool for benchmarking and promoting eco-efficiency in LLM deployments. The framework could be adopted by researchers, companies, and policymakers seeking to reduce the environmental impact of AI.

* **Justification for Score**

This paper is significant and novel. The methodology is detailed and justified and provides a means to assess the eco-efficiency of current LLMs across various infrastructure scenarios. There are acknowledged limitations such as the assumption of hardware configurations, which can impact the overall results. However, the integration of publicly accessible metrics and statistical inference offers a framework to approximate the environmental costs for systems which would otherwise be opaque. The case study of GPT-4's annual environmental footprint provides a concrete picture of the large scale effects of a resource heavy LLM.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 8/10

### Adversarial Suffix Filtering: a Defense Pipeline for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09602v1)
- **Authors**: David Khachaturov, Robert Mullins
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly embedded in autonomous systems and public-facing environments, yet they remain susceptible to jailbreak vulnerabilities that may undermine their security and trustworthiness. Adversarial suffixes are considered to be the current state-of-the-art jailbreak, consistently outperforming simpler methods and frequently succeeding even in black-box settings. Existing defenses rely on access to the internal architecture of models limiting diverse deployment, increase memory and computation footprints dramatically, or can be bypassed with simple prompt engineering methods. We introduce $\textbf{Adversarial Suffix Filtering}$ (ASF), a lightweight novel model-agnostic defensive pipeline designed to protect LLMs against adversarial suffix attacks. ASF functions as an input preprocessor and sanitizer that detects and filters adversarially crafted suffixes in prompts, effectively neutralizing malicious injections. We demonstrate that ASF provides comprehensive defense capabilities across both black-box and white-box attack settings, reducing the attack efficacy of state-of-the-art adversarial suffix generation methods to below 4%, while only minimally affecting the target model's capabilities in non-adversarial scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Adversarial Suffix Filtering: a Defense Pipeline for LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adversarial Suffix Filtering (ASF), a novel defense pipeline designed to protect Large Language Models (LLMs) against adversarial suffix attacks. ASF is a model-agnostic input preprocessor that detects and filters adversarially crafted suffixes in prompts, effectively neutralizing malicious injections.  The authors demonstrate that ASF significantly reduces the attack efficacy of state-of-the-art adversarial suffix generation methods across both black-box and white-box attack settings, while minimally affecting the target model's capabilities in non-adversarial scenarios. The approach leverages a text segmentation model (Segment Any Text) to divide prompts into segments and a fine-tuned BERT classifier to identify malicious segments. Post-processing heuristics are applied to refine the predictions and reduce false positives.  The pipeline is presented as a lightweight and practical solution for enhancing LLM security in resource-constrained environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of detecting and filtering adversarial suffixes is not entirely new. However, the specific implementation of ASF, combining a text segmentation model with a fine-tuned BERT classifier and post-processing heuristics in a model-agnostic pipeline, demonstrates a certain degree of innovation. The authors have effectively combined existing tools in a novel way to address a pressing problem. It appears that the approach and specific techniques and implementation are novel, even if the underlying principle (filtering malicious inputs) is not.

*   **Significance:** Prompt injection and adversarial suffixes represent a significant threat to LLM security and trustworthiness. Existing defenses often suffer from limitations such as model-specificity, high computational cost, or vulnerability to simple prompt engineering techniques. ASF offers a compelling alternative by providing a lightweight, model-agnostic, and effective defense mechanism. The reduction in attack success rate reported in the paper is substantial, indicating the potential for ASF to improve LLM security in real-world deployments. The relative ease of deployment as a front-end filter adds to the significance. The analysis of performance degradation (or lack thereof) on standard language tasks further strengthens the value of the work. The paper addresses an important problem, offers a practical and effective solution, and provides a thorough evaluation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Model-Agnostic Design:** The key strength of ASF is its ability to protect LLMs without requiring access to or modification of the underlying model architecture. This makes it broadly applicable to various LLMs, including those accessed via APIs.
    *   **Lightweight Implementation:** ASF's low computational overhead and memory requirements make it suitable for deployment in resource-constrained environments and latency-sensitive applications.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides a comprehensive empirical evaluation demonstrating ASF's effectiveness in neutralizing adversarial suffix attacks across different models and prompt sets.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:**  The authors analyze performance on common tasks to demonstrate minimal degradation, assess against multiple attack datasets, and evaluate against PPL defenses.
    * **Well articulated problem and contribution**

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Segmentation Accuracy:** ASF's effectiveness relies heavily on the accuracy of the text segmentation model. Imperfect segmentation can lead to misidentification of malicious suffixes or unintended removal of benign content.
    *   **Potential for False Positives:** While the post-processing heuristics aim to reduce false positives, there remains a risk that legitimate content might be mistakenly flagged as adversarial.
    *   **Limited Scope:** ASF is specifically designed to address adversarial suffix attacks and may not be effective against other types of prompt injection techniques.
    *   **Hand-tuned rules** The paper acknowledges that the need for hand-tuned rules for post-processing could require calibration for different contexts.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of LLM security by providing a practical and effective defense mechanism against adversarial suffix attacks. ASF's model-agnostic design and lightweight implementation make it a viable option for enhancing the security of LLMs in real-world deployments. Future research could focus on improving the robustness of ASF by enhancing the accuracy of the text segmentation model and exploring techniques for reducing false positives. Further work could investigate ASF in a mixed-context environment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LightLab: Controlling Light Sources in Images with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09608v1)
- **Authors**: Nadav Magar, Amir Hertz, Eric Tabellion, Yael Pritch, Alex Rav-Acha, Ariel Shamir, Yedid Hoshen
- **Abstract**: We present a simple, yet effective diffusion-based method for fine-grained, parametric control over light sources in an image. Existing relighting methods either rely on multiple input views to perform inverse rendering at inference time, or fail to provide explicit control over light changes. Our method fine-tunes a diffusion model on a small set of real raw photograph pairs, supplemented by synthetically rendered images at scale, to elicit its photorealistic prior for relighting. We leverage the linearity of light to synthesize image pairs depicting controlled light changes of either a target light source or ambient illumination. Using this data and an appropriate fine-tuning scheme, we train a model for precise illumination changes with explicit control over light intensity and color. Lastly, we show how our method can achieve compelling light editing results, and outperforms existing methods based on user preference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LightLab: Controlling Light Sources in Images with Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LightLab, a method for fine-grained, parametric control over light sources in single images using diffusion models.  The method addresses the challenge of explicitly controlling light intensity, color, and ambient illumination while maintaining physically plausible shadows and environmental effects. LightLab fine-tunes a diffusion model using a dataset composed of real photograph pairs (scene with light on/off) and a large-scale synthetically rendered 3D indoor scenes. It leverages the linearity of light to synthesize image pairs depicting controlled light changes. By conditioning the diffusion model on spatial information (input image, depth map, light source masks) and global controls (light intensity/color, ambient light intensity, tone-mapping strategy), LightLab provides a user with tools to precisely manipulate illumination within an image. Experiments demonstrate the method's ability to produce compelling light editing results and outperform existing methods in user preference studies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The primary novelty lies in achieving *explicit, parametric control* over light sources within a *single image* using a diffusion model.  Previous diffusion-based relighting methods often rely on textual prompts or lack precise control. Combining real and synthetic datasets is a reasonable approach to mitigate the scarcity of real-world training data and address the domain gap, however this is not particularly novel in itself.  The conditioning scheme is also an incremental, rather than groundbreaking, advancement. The use of depth maps and light source masks is intuitive, but their effective integration within a diffusion model constitutes some novelty.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance is that it offers a practical solution for a common and desirable image editing task – precisely controlling lighting after the photo has been taken.  This capability could be valuable in various applications, including content creation, post-production, and potentially even augmented reality.  The approach allows fine-grained control over the intensity and color of light, along with the ability to adjust ambient illumination.

**Strengths:**

*   **Explicit Control:** The method provides users with direct control over light source parameters, unlike many existing approaches relying on implicit representations.
*   **Data-Driven Approach:** Using a combination of real and synthetic data helps to create a robust model that generalizes better than approaches relying solely on synthetic data.
*   **Physically Plausible Results:** The paper highlights the method's ability to create physically plausible shadows and reflections, which is a crucial aspect of realistic relighting.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes both quantitative (PSNR, SSIM) and qualitative evaluations, including a user study, to demonstrate the method's effectiveness.
*  The model exhibits ability to extrapolate intensity change values and handle more general conditions that existing techniques might not address.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Bias:** The method is susceptible to dataset bias, as demonstrated by the candle example where the model inappropriately treats candles like light tubes.
*   **Limited Scene Diversity:** The paper acknowledges that the synthetic dataset has relatively low diversity in scene geometries and materials, which could limit the model's generalization ability.
*  **Reliance on Segmentation Masks:** The need for accurate segmentation masks (which in turn may require user input/assistance) can be seen as a bottleneck to the usability.
*  The model still occasionally demonstrates some perspective and lighting inaccuracies.
*  Relies on existing well-documented diffusion techniques and combines them into the model.

**Influence on the Field:**

The paper could influence the field by demonstrating the potential of diffusion models for achieving precise control over physical image properties. It might inspire future research in incorporating more physically-based constraints into generative models, developing more sophisticated conditioning schemes, and creating datasets with greater scene diversity.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of image editing, the novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking. The combination of real and synthetic data, the conditioning scheme, and the use of diffusion models for relighting are all based on existing techniques. However, the paper effectively combines these elements to achieve explicit and parametric control over light sources, which is a significant step forward. Given the dataset limitations and potential for generalization issues, the paper is innovative, yet limited in scope.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Customizing a Large Language Model for VHDL Design of High-Performance Microprocessors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.09610v1)
- **Authors**: Nicolas Dupuis, Ravi Nair, Shyam Ramji, Sean McClintock, Nishant Chauhan, Priyanka Nagpal, Bart Blaner, Ken Valk, Leon Stok, Ruchir Puri
- **Abstract**: The use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in hardware design has taken off in recent years, principally through its incorporation in tools that increase chip designer productivity. There has been considerable discussion about the use of LLMs in RTL specifications of chip designs, for which the two most popular languages are Verilog and VHDL. LLMs and their use in Verilog design has received significant attention due to the higher popularity of the language, but little attention so far has been given to VHDL despite its continued popularity in the industry. There has also been little discussion about the unique needs of organizations that engage in high-performance processor design, and techniques to deploy AI solutions in these settings. In this paper, we describe our journey in developing a Large Language Model (LLM) specifically for the purpose of explaining VHDL code, a task that has particular importance in an organization with decades of experience and assets in high-performance processor design. We show how we developed test sets specific to our needs and used them for evaluating models as we performed extended pretraining (EPT) of a base LLM. Expert evaluation of the code explanations produced by the EPT model increased to 69% compared to a base model rating of 43%. We further show how we developed an LLM-as-a-judge to gauge models similar to expert evaluators. This led us to deriving and evaluating a host of new models, including an instruction-tuned version of the EPT model with an expected expert evaluator rating of 71%. Our experiments also indicate that with the potential use of newer base models, this rating can be pushed to 85% and beyond. We conclude with a discussion on further improving the quality of hardware design LLMs using exciting new developments in the Generative AI world.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores customizing a Large Language Model (LLM) specifically for VHDL code explanation within a high-performance microprocessor design environment.  It addresses the gap in research compared to Verilog LLMs and the unique needs of organizations with legacy VHDL codebases. The authors detail their process of creating a custom LLM, including secure infrastructure setup, data acquisition and preprocessing, test set generation (code explanation and multiple-choice), extended pretraining (EPT) with domain-specific data, instruction tuning (IT), and model merging.  They also explore using an LLM-as-a-judge to automate model evaluation, overcoming limitations in human expert availability. The results demonstrate significant performance improvements with EPT and IT, and project potential gains with newer base models and techniques like beam search. The paper concludes with a discussion of deployment challenges, future improvements, and the potential impact on processor design cycles.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its focused application of LLMs to VHDL within a specific, real-world, high-performance processor design context. While LLMs for code generation are not entirely novel, the emphasis on VHDL, the detailed customization process, and the consideration of industrial-specific security constraints represent a valuable contribution. The LLM-as-a-judge aspect adds a practical dimension to automated evaluation in resource-constrained environments. The integration within secure environments, a critical need for semiconductor companies, adds significant practical value.
*   **Significance:** The work has the potential to significantly impact the productivity of VHDL-based hardware design teams, particularly in organizations with large legacy codebases and a need to onboard new engineers efficiently. The LLM can serve not only as a design aid but also as a vehicle for preserving and disseminating organizational design knowledge. The demonstration of improved performance through EPT and IT provides a tangible roadmap for other companies in similar situations.

**Strengths:**

*   **Real-world application:** The paper is grounded in a practical problem faced by many organizations in the semiconductor industry.
*   **Detailed methodology:** The authors provide a clear and detailed description of their LLM customization process, including data preparation, training techniques, and evaluation methods.
*   **Security considerations:** The paper explicitly addresses the crucial issue of data security in a high-value intellectual property environment.
*   **LLM-as-a-judge:** The exploration of an LLM-as-a-judge is a practical approach to scaling model evaluation with limited human resources.
*   **Quantitative results:** The paper provides quantitative results demonstrating the effectiveness of different techniques, along with a cost analysis.
*   **Future Directions:** The planned future improvements add a new direction of reasoning and inference scaling.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited generality:** The results are likely specific to the organization's VHDL codebase and design practices. The generalizability to other VHDL styles or different processor architectures is not thoroughly explored.
*   **Test set specificity:** The test set is tailored to the organization's domain expertise. While necessary for this setting, it makes comparison with other research more challenging.
*   **Base LLM Selection:** There is limited explanation of why *Granite* was selected as the base model versus other publicly available models.
*   **Lack of Comparison:** The paper would benefit from more explicit comparisons with existing VHDL-focused LLM tools or techniques, if any exist, to better contextualize the contribution.
*   **Limited User Feedback:** More data on feedback from users on the effectiveness of the models deployed would give more insights.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid application of LLMs to a relevant problem, with a strong emphasis on practicality and real-world constraints. The techniques employed are not entirely novel, but the specific application to VHDL in a secure, industrial environment, along with the detailed customization process and LLM-as-a-judge approach, justifies a good score. While the results might not be broadly generalizable, they offer a valuable case study for other organizations facing similar challenges. The quantitative results and consideration of security constraints add further value. The limitations in generality, detailed comparison and missing description of the base model selection deduct points.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10
